such that
|\mathbf{C}_{\alpha}(d)|_{U}>0
. As there is no majority color, we have
\mathbf{C}_{i}(d,Q)\leq\sum_{d^{\prime}\neq d}\mathbf{C}_{i}(d^{\prime},Q)
for every
i\in\mathbb{N}
. Let
\tau\geq\alpha
be given by
Lemma

9
and taken as minimal. By
Lemma

9
, for every
i\geq\tau
:

\mathrm{act}(\mathbf{C}_{i}(d))\subseteq Q_{M}\text{ and }\mathrm{act}(\mathbf{C}_{i}(d^{\prime}))\subseteq Q_{m}\text{ for all }d^{\prime}\neq d.

So, we have
|\mathbf{C}_{i}|_{Q_{M}}\leq|\mathbf{C}_{i}|_{Q_{m}}
for all
i\geq\tau
. By
Lemma

6
, this implies that
|\mathbf{C}_{i}|_{Q_{Y}}\leq|\mathbf{C}_{i}|_{Q_{N}}
for all
i\geq\tau
.

Let us show that the number of active states from
Q_{Y}
decreases
permanently to zero. Assume that there exist infinitely many indices

i\geq\tau
such that
|\mathbf{C}_{i}|_{Q_{Y}}>0
. As there is at least one state
q\in\mathrm{act}(\mathbf{C}_{i})\cap Q_{Y}
and
|\mathbf{C}_{i}|_{Q_{Y}}\leq|\mathbf{C}_{i}|_{Q_{N}}
, rule
(11)
is enabled. So, there exists
\mathbf{C}_{i}^{\prime}
such that
\mathbf{C}_{i}\xrightarrow{*}\mathbf{C}_{i}^{\prime}
and
|\mathbf{C}_{i}|_{Q_{Y}}>|\mathbf{C}_{i}^{\prime}|_{Q_{Y}}
. By repeatedly invoking fairness, we
obtain some
\tau^{\prime}\geq\tau
with
|\mathbf{C}_{\tau^{\prime}}|_{Q_{Y}}=0
. Let
\tau^{\prime}
be minimal. Rules
(11)
and
(12)
are now
disabled.

Since rule
(12)
is disabled, we have
|\mathbf{C}_{i}|_{Q_{y}}\geq|\mathbf{C}_{i+1}|_{Q_{y}}
for all
i\geq\tau^{\prime}
. Let us show that the
number of active states from
Q_{y}
decreases permanently to
zero. Two subcases arise.

First, consider the case where
|\mathbf{C}_{\tau^{\prime}}|_{Q_{N}}>0
. Assume that there exist infinitely many
i\geq\tau^{\prime}

such that
|\mathbf{C}_{i}|_{Q_{y}}>0
, as we are otherwise
done. Since there exists at least one state
q\in\mathrm{act}(\mathbf{C}_{i})\cap Q_{N}
, rule
(13)
is enabled. Therefore, there exists
\mathbf{C}_{i}^{\prime}
such
that
\mathbf{C}_{i}\xrightarrow{*}\mathbf{C}_{i}^{\prime}
and
|\mathbf{C}_{i}|_{Q_{y}}>|\mathbf{C}_{i}^{\prime}|_{Q_{y}}
. By repeatedly invoking fairness, we
obtain some
\tau^{\prime\prime}\geq\tau^{\prime}
such that
|\mathbf{C}_{\tau^{\prime\prime}}|_{Q_{y}}=0
. This disables rules
(13)
and
(14)
.

Let us now consider the remaining case where
|\mathbf{C}_{\tau^{\prime}}|_{Q_{N}}=0
. From
Lemma

6
, it follows that for any
v\geq\tau
,
|\mathbf{C}_{v}|_{Q_{M}}=|\mathbf{C}_{v}|_{Q_{m}}
. Since each agent is initially in a state
q
such that
\mathrm{grp}(q)
holds, since
|\mathbf{C}_{\alpha}|_{U}>0
and since
\varphi_{\text{maj}}(\mathbf{C}_{0})=\mathit{false}
, at least one agent has its group changed in the execution. There exists a transition
t=((p,q),\sim,(p^{\prime},q^{\prime}))
and data
d_{1},d_{2}\in\mathbb{D}
such that
(\mathrm{grp}(p)\neq\mathrm{grp}(p^{\prime}))\lor(\mathrm{grp}(q)\neq\mathrm{grp}(q^{\prime}))
holds,
\mathbf{C}_{\tau-1}\xrightarrow{t}\mathbf{C}_{\tau}
and
\mathbf{C}_{\tau}=\mathbf{C}_{\tau-1}-(\bm{p}_{d_{1}}+\bm{q}_{d_{2}})+(\bm{p^{\prime}}_{d_{1}}+\bm{q^{\prime}}_{d_{2}})
. Observe that
t
must be a transition arising from a rule
(2)
,
(3)
,
(6)
,
(7)
or
(8)
. We identify two subcases.

Subcase: transition
t
arises from rule
(2)
,
(3)
,
(6)
or
(7)
.
Recall that for any
v\geq\tau
, groups are stabilized in
\mathbf{C}_{v}
, and hence
|\mathbf{C}_{v}|_{Q_{Y}}=|\mathbf{C}_{v}|_{Q_{N}}
since
|\mathbf{C}_{v}|_{Q_{M}}=|\mathbf{C}_{v}|_{Q_{m}}
. We know from the definition of the rules that either
|\mathbf{C}_{\tau}|_{Q_{N}}>0
(which implies
|\mathbf{C}_{\tau}|_{Q_{Y}}>0
) or
|\mathbf{C}_{\tau}|_{Q_{Y}}>0
(which implies
|\mathbf{C}_{\tau}|_{Q_{N}}>0
). Therefore,

\mathbf{C}_{\tau^{\prime}-1}\xrightarrow{(11)}\mathbf{C}_{\tau^{\prime}}\text{ and }|\mathbf{C}_{\tau^{\prime}-1}|_{Q_{Y}}=|\mathbf{C}_{\tau^{\prime}-1}|_{Q_{N}}=1.

By definition of rule
(12)
, it follows that
|\mathbf{C}_{\tau^{\prime}}|_{Q_{n}}\geq 2
.

Subcase: transition
t
arises from rule
(8)
.

By definition of
(8)
, we have
|\mathbf{C}_{\tau}|_{Q_{n}}\geq 2
. If
|\mathbf{C}_{\tau}|_{Q_{Y}}=|\mathbf{C}_{\tau}|_{Q_{N}}=0
, then
\tau=\tau^{\prime}
. Otherwise, we have

|\mathbf{C}_{\tau}|_{Q_{Y}}=|\mathbf{C}_{\tau}|_{Q_{N}}>0\text{ and }\mathbf{C}_{\tau^{\prime}-1}\xrightarrow{(11)}\mathbf{C}_{\tau^{\prime}}.

Again, it follows that
|\mathbf{C}_{\tau^{\prime}}|_{Q_{n}}\geq 2
.

In both subcases, we have
|\mathbf{C}_{\tau^{\prime}}|_{Q_{n}}>0
. Observe that rule
(13)
is disabled. Thus, only
rule
(14)
can now change a state
q
to a state
q^{\prime}
such
that
\mathrm{maj}(q^{\prime})\neq\mathrm{maj}(q)
. Since
|\mathbf{C}_{\tau^{\prime}}|_{Q_{n}}>0
,
rule
(14)
is enabled for any
j\geq\tau^{\prime}
such that

|\mathbf{C}_{j}|_{Q_{y}}>0
. So, by repeatedly
invoking fairness, we obtain some
\tau^{\prime\prime}\geq\tau^{\prime}
such that

|\mathbf{C}_{\tau^{\prime\prime}}|_{Q_{y}}=0
.

Using the results shown in the proof of
Lemma

11
, we know there are no active states
q
in
\mathbf{C}_{\tau}
(and in any
\mathbf{C}^{\prime}
reachable from
\mathbf{C}_{\tau}
) with
\mathrm{maj}(q)\in\{\overline{Y},\overline{N}\}
. Thus, we are
done, as
\mathrm{maj}(q)\in\{N,n\}
holds for every
i\geq\tau^{\prime\prime}
and

q\in\mathrm{act}(\mathbf{C}_{i})
.
∎

Appendix B
Missing proofs of Section
4.1

Lemma A1

(monotonicity)
.

Let
\mathbf{C}
,
\overline{\mathbf{C}}
and
\mathbf{C}^{\prime}
be configurations such that
\mathbf{C}\xrightarrow{*}\overline{\mathbf{C}}
and
\mathbf{C}\sqsubseteq\mathbf{C}^{\prime}
. There exists

\overline{\mathbf{C}^{\prime}}
such that
\mathbf{C}^{\prime}\xrightarrow{*}\overline{\mathbf{C}^{\prime}}
and

\overline{\mathbf{C}}\sqsubseteq\overline{\mathbf{C}^{\prime}}
.

Proof.

We consider the case where
\mathbf{C}\xrightarrow{t}\overline{\mathbf{C}}
. The general
case “
{\xrightarrow{*}}
” follows by induction. Let
\rho\colon\mathbb{D}\to\mathbb{D}
be an injection such that
\mathbf{C}(x)\leq\mathbf{C}^{\prime}(\rho(x))
for all
x\in\mathbb{D}
. Let
t=((p,q),\sim,(p^{\prime},q^{\prime}))
be the transition used
in
\mathbf{C}
with
d,e\in\mathbb{D}
. As
\mathbf{C}\geq\bm{p}_{d}+\bm{q}_{e}
, we have
\mathbf{C}^{\prime}\geq\bm{p}_{\rho(d)}+\bm{q}_{\rho(e)}
. So, we can use
t
in
\mathbf{C}^{\prime}
to
obtain
\overline{\mathbf{C}^{\prime}}\coloneqq\mathbf{C}^{\prime}-(\bm{p}_{\rho(d)}+\bm{q}_{\rho(e)})+(\bm{p^{\prime}}_{\rho(d)}+\bm{q^{\prime}}_{\rho(e)})
. We have

\overline{\mathbf{C}}(d)\leq\overline{\mathbf{C}^{\prime}}(\rho(x))
for all
x\in\mathbb{D}
,
and hence
\mathbf{C}^{\prime}\sqsubseteq\overline{\mathbf{C}^{\prime}}
.
∎

Lemma A2
.

The set
\mathcal{U}
is upward closed and has a finite basis.

Proof.

It suffices to show that
\mathcal{U}
is upward closed. Indeed, since

\sqsubseteq
is a well-quasi-order (
e.g.
, see
[
18
]
), it
follows that
\mathcal{U}
has a finite basis.

Let
\mathbf{C}
and
\mathbf{C}^{\prime}
be configurations such that
\mathbf{C}\in\mathcal{U}
and
\mathbf{C}\sqsubseteq\mathbf{C}^{\prime}
. If
O(\mathbf{C}^{\prime})=\bot
, then we are done. Therefore,
assume that
O(\mathbf{C}^{\prime})=b\in\{0,1\}
. Since
\mathrm{act}(\mathbf{C})\subseteq\mathrm{act}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime})
, we have
O(\mathbf{C})=b
. As
\mathbf{C}\in\mathcal{U}
, there exists a
configuration
\overline{\mathbf{C}}
such that
\mathbf{C}\xrightarrow{*}\overline{\mathbf{C}}

and
O(\overline{\mathbf{C}})\neq b
. By
1
, there
exists
\overline{\mathbf{C}^{\prime}}
such that
\mathbf{C}^{\prime}\xrightarrow{*}\overline{\mathbf{C}^{\prime}}
and

\overline{\mathbf{C}}\sqsubseteq\overline{\mathbf{C}^{\prime}}
. As
\mathrm{act}(\overline{\mathbf{C}})\subseteq\mathrm{act}(\overline{\mathbf{C}^{\prime}})
, we either have
O(\overline{\mathbf{C}^{\prime}})=O(\overline{\mathbf{C}})
or
O(\overline{\mathbf{C}^{\prime}})=\bot
. So, we obtain
\mathbf{C}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{U}
.
∎

Lemma A3
.

Let
k\geq 1
and let
\mathbf{C},\mathbf{C}^{\prime}
be configurations such that
\mathbf{C}\sqsubseteq\mathbf{C}^{\prime}
. It is the case that
\tau_{k}(\mathbf{C})\sqsubseteq\mathbf{C}
and

\tau_{k}(\mathbf{C})\sqsubseteq\tau_{k}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime})
.

Proof.

For every
d\in\mathbb{D}
and
q\in Q
, we have
\tau_{k}(\mathbf{C})(d,q)=\min(\mathbf{C}(d,q),k)\leq\mathbf{C}(d,q)
. Thus,
\tau_{k}(\mathbf{C})\sqsubseteq\mathbf{C}

trivially holds (using the identity function).

Let
\rho\colon\mathbb{D}\to\mathbb{D}
be an injection such that
\mathbf{C}(d)\leq\mathbf{C}^{\prime}(\rho(d))
for every
d\in\mathbb{D}
. For every
d\in\mathbb{D}
and
q\in Q
, we have

\tau_{k}(\mathbf{C})(d,q)=\min(\mathbf{C}(d,q),k)\leq\min(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}(\rho(d),q),k)=\tau_{k}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime})(\rho(d),q).

Thus,
\tau_{k}(\mathbf{C})\sqsubseteq\tau_{k}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime})
(using injection
\rho
).
∎

See
14

Proof.

By
2
, the set
\mathcal{U}
has a finite basis
\{\mathbf{C}_{1},\mathbf{C}_{2},\ldots,\mathbf{C}_{n}\}
. Let
k\coloneqq\max\{\mathbf{C}_{i}(d,q):d\in\mathbb{D},q\in Q\}
. Note that
\tau_{k}(\mathbf{C}_{i})=\mathbf{C}_{i}
for all
i\in[1..n]
. Let us first show that
\mathbf{C}\in\mathcal{U}
iff
\tau_{k}(\mathbf{C})\in\mathcal{U}
.

\Rightarrow
) As
\mathbf{C}\in\mathcal{U}
, we have
\mathbf{C}_{i}\sqsubseteq\mathbf{C}
for some
i\in[1..n]
. By
3
, we have
\tau_{k}(\mathbf{C}_{i})\sqsubseteq\tau_{k}(\mathbf{C})
. Since
\mathbf{C}_{i}=\tau_{k}(\mathbf{C}_{i})
, we obtain
that
\mathbf{C}_{i}\sqsubseteq\tau_{k}(\mathbf{C})
and hence
\tau_{k}(\mathbf{C})\in\mathcal{U}
.

\Leftarrow
) Since
\tau_{k}(\mathbf{C})\in\mathcal{U}
, we have
\mathbf{C}_{i}\sqsubseteq\tau_{k}(\mathbf{C})
for some
i\in[1..n]
. By
3
, we
have
\tau_{k}(\mathbf{C})\sqsubseteq\mathbf{C}
. Consequently,
\mathbf{C}_{i}\sqsubseteq\mathbf{C}
, which
implies
\mathbf{C}\in\mathcal{U}
.

It remains to prove the main claim. As
\mathcal{S}_{0}\cup\mathcal{S}_{1}
is the
complement of
\mathcal{U}
, we have
\mathbf{C}\in\mathcal{S}_{0}\cup\mathcal{S}_{1}
iff
\tau_{k}(\mathbf{C})\in\mathcal{S}_{0}\cup\mathcal{S}_{1}
. Moreover,
O(\mathbf{C})=O(\tau_{k}(\mathbf{C}))
since
\mathrm{act}(\mathbf{C})=\mathrm{act}(\tau_{k}(\mathbf{C}))
. Therefore,
\mathbf{C}\in\mathcal{S}_{b}
iff
\tau_{k}(\mathbf{C})\in\mathcal{S}_{b}
.
∎

See
15

Proof.

Let
\mathbf{C}_{0}\mathbf{C}_{1}\ldots\mathbf{C}_{m}
be a fair execution reaching a stable configuration
\mathbf{C}_{m}
with output
b\in\{0,1\}
. Let
s_{i}(j)
be the state of the
j
-th agent of datum
d
in configuration
\mathbf{C}_{i}
. Let
a
be such that
s_{0}(a)=q
and
\mathbf{C}_{m}(d,s_{m}(a))\geq k
, that is an agent initially in state
q
that ends up in state
s_{m}(a)=q^{\prime}
with at least
k
agents in
\mathbf{C}_{m}
. Note that since
\mathbf{C}_{0}(d,q)\geq\ell=|Q|\cdot(k-1)+1
, by the pigeonhole principle, such a state
q^{\prime}
must exist. We first show by induction that
\mathbf{C}_{0}+\bm{q}_{d}\xrightarrow{*}\mathbf{C}_{i}+\bm{s_{i}(a)}_{d}
.

Case
i=0
. Since
s_{0}(a)=q
and
a
is of datum
d
, it follows that
\mathbf{C}_{0}+\bm{q}_{d}=\mathbf{C}_{0}+\bm{s_{0}(a)}_{d}
, which implies that
\mathbf{C}_{0}+\bm{q}_{d}\xrightarrow{*}\mathbf{C}_{0}+\bm{s_{0}(a)}_{d}
.

Case
i>0
. Assume
\mathbf{C}_{0}+\bm{q}_{d}\xrightarrow{*}\mathbf{C}_{i-1}+\bm{s_{i-1}(a)}_{d}
. If
s_{i-1}(a)=s_{i}(a)
, then

\displaystyle\mathbf{C}_{0}+\bm{q}_{d}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{*}\mathbf{C}_{i-1}+\bm{s_{i-1}(a)}_{d}

(by induction hypothesis)

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{}\mathbf{C}_{i}+\bm{s_{i-1}(a)}_{d}

\displaystyle(\mathbf{C}_{i-1}\xrightarrow{}\mathbf{C}_{i})

\displaystyle=\mathbf{C}_{i}+\bm{s_{i}(a)}_{d}

\displaystyle(s_{i-1}(a)=s_{i}(a))

If
s_{i-1}(a)\neq s_{i}(a)
, there exist some
p\in Q
and some transition
t=((p,s_{i-1}(a)),\sim,(p,s_{i}(a)))
such that
\mathbf{C}_{i-1}\xrightarrow{t}\mathbf{C}_{i}
.
Since the protocol is immediate observation, this transition leaves the observed agent intact in
\mathbf{C}_{i}
, that is
\mathbf{C}_{i}\geq\bm{p}_{d^{\prime}}
for some
d^{\prime}\in\mathbb{D}
. This agent can again be observed by the agent of datum
d
in state
s_{i-1}(a)
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\mathbf{C}_{0}+\bm{q_{d}}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{*}\mathbf{C}_{i-1}+\bm{s_{i-1}(a)}_{d}

(by induction hypothesis)

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{t}\mathbf{C}_{i}+\bm{s_{i-1}(a)}_{d}

\displaystyle(\mathbf{C}_{i-1}\xrightarrow{}\mathbf{C}_{i})

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{t}\mathbf{C}_{i}+\bm{s_{i}(a)}_{d}

(by observing agent of
d^{\prime}
in state
p
)

Now, assume that
\mathbf{C}_{m}+\bm{q^{\prime}}_{d}\in\mathcal{U}
. We have

\displaystyle\mathbf{C}_{m}+\bm{q^{\prime}}_{d}\in\mathcal{U}

\displaystyle\implies\tau_{k}(\mathbf{C}_{m}+\bm{q^{\prime}}_{d})\in\mathcal{U}

(by
Lemma

14
)

\displaystyle\implies\tau_{k}(\mathbf{C}_{m})\in\mathcal{U}

\displaystyle(\mathbf{C}_{m}(d,q^{\prime})\geq k)

\displaystyle\implies\mathbf{C}_{m}\in\mathcal{U}

(by
Lemma

14
)

This is a contradiction to our assumption that
\mathbf{C}_{m}\in\mathcal{S}
. Therefore,
\mathbf{C}_{m}+\bm{q^{\prime}}_{d}
must be stable. Observe that since
\mathbf{C}_{m}(q^{\prime})>0
, it follows that
O(\mathbf{C}_{m}+\bm{q^{\prime}}_{d})=b=O(\mathbf{C}_{m})
. This implies that an execution starting from
\mathbf{C}_{0}+\bm{q}_{d}
reaches a stable configuration with output
b
. Therefore,
\psi(\mathbf{C}_{0})=\psi(\mathbf{C}_{0}+\bm{q}_{d})
.
∎

See
16

Proof.

Let
d,d^{\prime}
be as described and let
\mathbf{C}=\mathbf{C}_{0}\xrightarrow{t_{1}}\mathbf{C}_{1}\xrightarrow{t_{2}}\cdots\xrightarrow{t_{i}}\mathbf{C}_{i}=\mathbf{C}^{\prime}
. We prove the claim by
induction on
i
. As the claim trivially holds for
i=0
, let us
consider the case where
n>i
. We make a case distinction.

Case
\mathbf{C}_{i}(d)=\mathbf{C}_{i-1}(d)
. We have

\displaystyle\mathbf{C}_{0}+(\mathbf{C}_{0}(d))_{d^{\prime}}\

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{*}\

\displaystyle\mathbf{C}_{i-1}+(\mathbf{C}_{i-1}(d))_{d^{\prime}}\

(by induction hypothesis)

\displaystyle=\

\displaystyle\mathbf{C}_{i-1}+(\mathbf{C}_{i}(d))_{d^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{}\

\displaystyle\mathbf{C}_{i}+(\mathbf{C}_{i}(d))_{d^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\quad\text{(by $\mathbf{C}_{i-1}\xrightarrow{}\mathbf{C}_{i}$)}.

Case
\mathbf{C}_{i}(d)\neq\mathbf{C}_{i-1}(d)
.
We show that

(\mathbf{C}_{i-1}(d))_{d^{\prime}}\xrightarrow{t}(\mathbf{C}_{i}(d))_{d^{\prime}}
. By assumption, there

. The latter implies

. Let

{\sim}
.

•

is “
=
”. If
p=q
, we have
\mathbf{C}_{i-1}(d,q)\geq 2
. Thus, we have

\mathbf{C}_{i-1}(d,q)\geq 1
. This also implies that
(\mathbf{C}_{i-1}(d))_{d^{\prime}}\xrightarrow{t}(\mathbf{C}_{i}(d))_{d^{\prime}}

•

Assume that
\sim
is “
\neq
”. Some color
d^{\prime\prime}\in\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{C}_{0})

p\in\mathrm{act}(\mathbf{C}_{i-1}(d^{\prime\prime}))

p\in\mathrm{act}(\mathbf{C}_{i}(d^{\prime\prime}))

. ∎

See
17

Proof.

Let
\bm{f}
be a form with
\mathrm{act}(\bm{f})\subseteq I
, let
\{\mathbf{C}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{C}_{n}\}
be a basis of
\mathcal{U}
given by
2
, and let
m\coloneqq\max(|\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{C}_{1})|,|\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{C}_{2})|,\ldots,|\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{C}_{n})|)
. We define
h(\bm{f})
as follows:

h(\bm{f})\coloneqq(m-1)\cdot|Q|^{\sum_{q\in Q}\bm{f}(q)}+1.

Let
\mathbf{C}_{0}
be an initial configuration such that
\#_{\bm{f}}(\mathbf{C}_{0})\geq h(\bm{f})
. Let
b\coloneqq\psi(\mathbf{C}_{0})
. Let
\mathbf{C}\in\mathcal{S}_{b}
be such that

\mathbf{C}_{0}\xrightarrow{*}\mathbf{C}
. By the pigeonhole principle, there exists
d\in\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{C}_{0})
such that

•

\mathbf{C}_{0}(d)=\bm{f}
, and

•

\mathbf{C}(d)=\bm{f}^{\prime}
with
\#_{\bm{f}^{\prime}}(\mathbf{C})\geq m
.

Let us now inject the form
\bm{f}
with a new datum
d^{\prime}\in\mathbb{D}\setminus\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{C}_{0})
. By Lemma
16
, we have

\mathbf{C}_{0}+\bm{f}_{d^{\prime}}=\mathbf{C}_{0}+(\mathbf{C}_{0}(d))_{d^{\prime}}\xrightarrow{*}\mathbf{C}+(\mathbf{C}(d))_{d^{\prime}}=\mathbf{C}+\bm{f}^{\prime}_{d^{\prime}}.

Let
\mathbf{C}^{\prime}\coloneqq\mathbf{C}+\bm{f}^{\prime}_{d^{\prime}}
. As
\mathbf{C}(d)=\bm{f}^{\prime}
and
\mathbf{C}\in\mathcal{S}_{b}
,
we have
O(\mathbf{C})=O(\bm{f}^{\prime}_{d^{\prime}})=b
and so
O(\mathbf{C}^{\prime})=b
.

Let us now show that
\mathbf{C}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}_{b}
. For the sake of contradiction,
suppose that
\mathbf{C}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{U}
. There exists
i\in[1..n]
such that

\mathbf{C}_{i}\sqsubseteq\mathbf{C}^{\prime}
. By the latter, there exists an injection
\rho\colon\mathbb{D}\to\mathbb{D}
such that
\mathbf{C}_{i}(x)\leq\mathbf{C}^{\prime}(\rho(x))
for every
x\in\mathbb{D}
. Since

\#_{\bm{f}^{\prime}}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime})>\#_{\bm{f}^{\prime}}(\mathbf{C})\geq m\geq|\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{C}_{i})|,

d^{*}\in\mathbb{D}
such that
\mathbf{C}^{\prime}(d^{*})=\bm{f}^{\prime}
, and either

d^{*}
does not belong to the image of
\rho
or

\mathbf{C}_{i}(\rho^{-1}(d^{*}))=\bm{0}
. Let us show that
\mathbf{C}_{i}\sqsubseteq\mathbf{C}
, which yields the contradiction
\mathbf{C}\in\mathcal{U}
. We make a case
distinction.

Case
\rho^{-1}(d^{\prime})
undefined
. We trivially have

\mathbf{C}_{i}(x)\leq\mathbf{C}^{\prime}(\rho(x))=\mathbf{C}(\rho(x))
for all
x\in\mathbb{D}
.

Case
d^{*}=d^{\prime}
. Since
\mathbf{C}_{i}(\rho^{-1}(d^{\prime}))=\mathbf{C}_{i}(\rho^{-1}(d^{*}))=\bm{0}=\mathbf{C}(d^{\prime})=\mathbf{C}(d^{*})
, and as
\mathbf{C}
and

\mathbf{C}^{\prime}
only differ on
d^{\prime}
, we have
\mathbf{C}_{i}(x)\leq\mathbf{C}(\rho(x))
for
every
x\in\mathbb{D}
. Thus,
\mathbf{C}_{i}\sqsubseteq\mathbf{C}
.

Case
d^{*}\neq d^{\prime}
. Let
\rho^{\prime}\colon\mathbb{D}\to\mathbb{D}
be
the injection given by
\rho
, but with
d^{*}
playing the role of

d^{\prime}
(and possibly the converse to ensure that
\rho^{\prime}
is indeed an
injection),
i.e.

\rho^{\prime}(x)\coloneqq\begin{cases}d^{\prime}&\text{if }\rho^{-1}(d^{*})\text{ is defined and }x=\rho^{-1}(d^{*}),\\
d^{*}&\text{if }x=\rho^{-1}(d^{\prime}),\\
\rho(x)&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

As
d^{*}\neq d^{\prime}
, we have
\mathbf{C}(d^{*})=\mathbf{C}^{\prime}(d^{*})=\bm{f}^{\prime}
. Thus,

\mathbf{C}_{i}(\rho^{-1}(d^{\prime}))\leq\mathbf{C}^{\prime}(d^{\prime})=\bm{f}^{\prime}=\mathbf{C}(d^{*})=\mathbf{C}(\rho^{\prime}(\rho^{-1}(d^{\prime}))).

Furthermore, if
\rho^{-1}(d^{*})
is defined, then we have

\mathbf{C}_{i}(\rho^{-1}(d^{*}))=\bm{0}=\mathbf{C}(d^{\prime})=\mathbf{C}(\rho^{\prime}(\rho^{-1}(d^{*})))
. Thus, since
\mathbf{C}
and
\mathbf{C}^{\prime}
only differ
on datum
d^{\prime}
, we have
\mathbf{C}_{i}(x)\leq\mathbf{C}(\rho^{\prime}(x))
for all
x\in\mathbb{D}
, which implies
\mathbf{C}_{i}\sqsubseteq\mathbf{C}
.
∎

Proof.

We prove the two parts of the claim independently. The validity of
the claim follows by taking the maximum among the two thresholds

\tau
.

Threshold for
Q_{L}
. Note that
\mathbf{C}\xrightarrow{(1)}\mathbf{C}^{\prime}

implies
|\mathbf{C}^{\prime}|_{Q_{L}}=|\mathbf{C}|_{Q_{L}}-1>0
, and
|\mathbf{C}|_{Q_{L}}=|\mathbf{C}^{\prime}|_{Q_{L}}

otherwise. Therefore, there exist
\tau\in\mathbb{N}
and
k\geq 1
such
that
|\mathbf{C}_{\tau}|_{Q_{L}}=|\mathbf{C}_{\tau+1}|_{Q_{L}}=\cdots=k
. If
k=1
, then we are done. Thus, for the sake of
contradiction, assume that
k\geq 2
. Rule
(1)
is enabled
in each of
\mathbf{C}_{\tau},\mathbf{C}_{\tau+1},\ldots
. By fairness, this implies
that for some
\tau^{\prime}\geq\tau
, we have
|\mathbf{C}_{\tau^{\prime}}|_{Q_{L}}>|\mathbf{C}_{\tau^{\prime}+1}|_{Q_{L}}
, which is a contradiction.

Threshold for
Q_{C}
. The proof is the same, but
using rule
(2)
.
∎

See
22

Proof.

Note that rule
(3)
is the only rule modifying the value of a state; it requires two states with the same value and it increases the value of a single state by
1
.
Therefore, for any
\mathbf{C},\mathbf{C}^{\prime}
and
d\in\mathbb{D}
such that
\mathbf{C}\xrightarrow{}\mathbf{C}^{\prime}
with
\mathbf{C}^{\prime}=\mathbf{C}-\bm{q}_{d}+\bm{q^{\prime}}_{d}
for some states
q,q^{\prime}\in Q
, either
\mathrm{val}(q^{\prime})=\mathrm{val}(q)
, or
\mathrm{val}(q^{\prime})=\mathrm{val}(q)+1

holds. Further
observe that no rule changes a state
q
to a state
q^{\prime}
such that

\mathrm{elem}(q^{\prime})\neq\mathrm{elem}(q)
,
i.e.

\mathrm{elem}(q)
is immutable for any
q
.

Let
d\in\mathbb{D}
and
j\in[1..m]
be such that
\mathbf{C}_{0}(d,Q_{j})>0
. We define

Q_{j,k}\coloneqq\{q\in Q:\mathrm{elem}(q)=j\land\mathrm{val}(q)=k\}\text{ and }Q_{j,>k}\coloneqq\bigcup_{k^{\prime}=k+1}^{r}Q_{j,k^{\prime}}.

We claim that, for every
1\leq k<\min(\mathbf{C}_{0}(d,Q_{j})+1,r)
,
there exists
\tau\in\mathbb{N}
such that, for every
\tau^{\prime}\geq\tau
,
the following holds:

|\mathbf{C}_{\tau^{\prime}}(d)|_{Q_{j,k}}=1\text{ and }|\mathbf{C}_{\tau^{\prime}}(d)|_{Q_{j,>k}}=\mathbf{C}_{0}(d,Q_{j})-k.

Before proving the claim, let us see how it allows to conclude. If

\mathbf{C}_{0}(d,Q_{j})\leq r
, then this confirms the existence of an agent of
d

in a
(d,j)
-valid state. Note that if
k=r-1
, then
Q_{j,>k}
is in fact
Q_{j,r}
. Therefore, if
\mathbf{C}_{0}(d,Q_{j})>r
, then
agents will accumulate in some states of
Q_{j,r}
. In that case,
there will be more than one agent of
d
in a
(d,j)
-valid state. Let us now prove the claim by induction on
k
.

Base case
(k=1)
. Since all states
q\in I
satisfy
\mathrm{val}(q)=1
, we gather from the previous observations that for
\mathbf{C}_{i},\mathbf{C}^{\prime}
such that
\mathbf{C}_{i}\xrightarrow{*}\mathbf{C}^{\prime}
,
\mathbf{C}_{i}(d,Q_{j,1})\geq\mathbf{C}^{\prime}(d,Q_{j,1})
holds. Note that the fact that two states with the same value are needed for rule
(3)
to decrease
\mathbf{C}^{\prime}(d,Q_{j,1})
implies that
\mathbf{C}^{\prime}(d,Q_{j,1})\geq 1
. Note also that for any
q\in\mathrm{act}(\mathbf{C}_{i})
, either
\mathrm{val}(q)=1
or
\mathrm{val}(q)>1
. By the above observations,
there exist
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
and
\kappa\geq 1
such that
|\mathbf{C}_{\ell}(d)|_{Q_{j,1}}=|\mathbf{C}_{\ell+1}(d)|_{Q_{j,1}}=\cdots=\kappa
. If
\kappa=1
, then
|\mathbf{C}_{\ell}(d)|_{Q_{j,>1}}=\mathbf{C}_{0}(d,Q_{j})-1
and we are
done. Otherwise, rule
(3)
is enabled in each of
\mathbf{C}_{\ell},\mathbf{C}_{\ell+1},\ldots
. By fairness, this implies the existence of

\ell^{\prime}\geq\ell
such that

|\mathbf{C}_{\ell^{\prime}}(d)|_{Q_{j,1}}>|\mathbf{C}_{\ell^{\prime}+1}(d)|_{Q_{j,1}}\text{ and }|\mathbf{C}_{\ell^{\prime}}(d)|_{Q_{j,>1}}<|\mathbf{C}_{\ell^{\prime}+1}(d)|_{Q_{j,>1}},

which is a contradiction.

Induction step
. Assume that the claim is true for

k
such that
1<k<r-1
and
k\leq\mathbf{C}_{0}(d,Q_{j})
. Let us show
that the claim holds for
k+1
. If
k=\mathbf{C}_{0}(d,Q_{j})
, then
k+1\not<\min(\mathbf{C}_{0}(d,Q_{j})+1,r)
, and hence we are trivially done as
the claim does not have to hold for
k+1
. Thus, let us assume
that
k<\mathbf{C}_{0}(d,Q_{j})
. By induction hypothesis, there exists
v\in\mathbb{N}
such that, for every
v^{\prime}\geq v
, the following holds:

|\mathbf{C}_{v^{\prime}}(d)|_{Q_{j,k}}=1\text{ and }|\mathbf{C}_{v^{\prime}}(d)|_{Q_{j,>k}}=\mathbf{C}_{0}(d,Q_{j})-k.

Since values are incremented by
1
(
i.e.
there are no gaps in
values), at least one agent is in a state of
Q_{j,k+1}
. Suppose
that for some
w\geq v
, there exist states
p,q\in Q_{j,k+1}

such that
\mathbf{C}_{w}\geq\bm{p}_{d}+\bm{q}_{d}
. Then, rule
(3)

is enabled, implying the existence of a configuration
\mathbf{C}^{\prime}
such
that
\mathbf{C}_{w}\xrightarrow{*}\mathbf{C}^{\prime}
,

|\mathbf{C}_{w}(d)|_{Q_{j,k+1}}>|\mathbf{C}^{\prime}(d)|_{Q_{j,k+1}}\text{ and }|\mathbf{C}_{w}(d)|_{Q_{j,>k+1}}<|\mathbf{C}^{\prime}(d)|_{Q_{j,>k+1}}.

By fairness, this implies the existence of some
w^{\prime}
with

|\mathbf{C}_{w^{\prime}}(d)|_{Q_{j,k+1}}=1
. We are done since

\displaystyle|\mathbf{C}_{w^{\prime}}(d)|_{Q_{j,>k+1}}

\displaystyle=|\mathbf{C}_{w^{\prime}}(d)|_{Q_{j,>k}}-|\mathbf{C}_{w^{\prime}}(d)|_{Q_{j,k+1}}

(by definition of
Q_{j,\cdot}
)

\displaystyle=|\mathbf{C}_{w^{\prime}}(d)|_{Q_{j,>k}}-1

\displaystyle=(\mathbf{C}_{0}(d,Q_{j})-k)-1

\displaystyle=\mathbf{C}_{0}(d,Q_{j})-(k+1).

Proof.

Note that only rule
(4)
changes a state
q

q^{\prime}

\mathrm{count}_{j}(q^{\prime})\neq\mathrm{count}_{j}(q)

in

Proof.

Initially, each state
q\in\mathrm{act}(\mathbf{C}_{0})
satisfies
\mathrm{role}(q)=0
.
Observe that only rule
(5)
can assign a strictly positive
role to a state. Formally, for a datum
d\in\mathbb{D}
, a role
i\in[1..n]
and configurations
\mathbf{C}_{\tau},\mathbf{C}^{\prime}
such that
\mathbf{C}_{\tau}\xrightarrow{t}\mathbf{C}^{\prime}
and
\mathbf{C}^{\prime}=\mathbf{C}_{\tau}-\bm{q}_{d}+\bm{q^{\prime}}_{d}
for some
q,q^{\prime}\in Q
and transition
t
arising from rule
(5)
, the
leader’s state
q
must satisfy
\mathrm{count}_{j}(q)\in T(i,j)
for all
j\in[1..m]
. Thus, a leader must have the correct counts for a role
before self-assigning the aforementioned role.

Futher observe that rule
(4)
is the only rule changing a
count for a leader. Formally, for a datum
d\in\mathbb{D}
, a role
i\in[1..n]
and configurations
\mathbf{C}_{\tau},\mathbf{C}^{\prime}
such that
\mathbf{C}_{\tau}\xrightarrow{t}\mathbf{C}^{\prime}
and
\mathbf{C}^{\prime}=\mathbf{C}_{\tau}-\bm{q}_{d}+\bm{q^{\prime}}_{d}
for some
q,q^{\prime}\in Q
and transition
t
arising from rule
(4)
, this
leader must enter a new state
q^{\prime}
such that
\mathrm{role}(q^{\prime})<0
if it
no longer satisfies the count condition for the corresponding
interval. Therefore, after updating its count, the leader either has
a strictly negative assigned role or it still satisfies the role it
previously assumed.

Therefore, for any
\tau\in\mathbb{N}
, all leaders with strictly positive
roles have the correct counts for their role in
\mathbf{C}_{\tau}
.
∎

Lemma A4
.

For every
\tau\in\mathbb{N}

Proof.

Observe that only rule
(5)
can assign a strictly positive
role to a state. Formally, for a datum
d\in\mathbb{D}
and configurations

\mathbf{C}_{\tau},\mathbf{C}^{\prime}
such that
\mathbf{C}_{\tau}\xrightarrow{(5)}\mathbf{C}^{\prime}
and
\mathbf{C}^{\prime}=\mathbf{C}_{\tau}-\bm{q}_{d}+\bm{q^{\prime}}_{d}

, state
q

\mathrm{lead}(q)
. Therefore, non-leaders are never directly assigned a

Further note that only rule
(1)
removes leadership from a
state. Formally, for a datum
d\in\mathbb{D}
and configurations
\mathbf{C}_{\tau},\mathbf{C}^{\prime}

\mathbf{C}_{\tau}\xrightarrow{(1)}\mathbf{C}^{\prime}

\mathbf{C}^{\prime}=\mathbf{C}_{\tau}-\bm{q}_{d}+\bm{q^{\prime}}_{d}

q^{\prime}
must satisfy
\mathrm{role}(q^{\prime})<0
. Hence, non-leaders are
assigned a strictly negative role when their leadership is stripped
away. Note that leadership removal happens only once.

Therefore, non-leaders can never have a strictly positive assigned
role.

Proof of
\Rightarrow
).

By definition,
\mathbf{C}_{\beta}
has a unique controller elected and a
unique leader for each datum, carrying the correct counts for its
datum’s initial states. Since
\psi(\mathbf{C}_{0})
is
\mathit{true}
, a subset of
these leaders can fulfill the
n
roles simultaneously. While the
roles might not be correctly distributed at
\mathbf{C}_{\beta}
, we show that
for some configuration
\mathbf{C}
reachable from
\mathbf{C}_{\beta}
, the

\mathit{true}
. Let us consider the
following lemmas and their implications:

•

from
Lemma

24
, a leader with an assigned role always
thinks
it can currently fill this role,

•

from
Lemma

26
, all non-leaders eventually have their roles set to
0
, leaving only the elected leaders with (possibly) assigned roles,

•

from
Lemma

27
and
Lemma

28
, eventually, the unique controller can only have a task
i
set to
\mathit{true}
if a leader has its role set to
\pm i
.

Therefore, for configuration
\mathbf{C}_{\gamma}
, only leaders have a non-zero role. These leaders also correctly
think
they can fulfill the roles to which they are assigned. Finally, there is a unique controller and no agent is in a negative controller state.

We now define a
valid assignment

A
as an injective function
A\colon[1..n]\to\mathbb{D}
such that if
A(i)=d
, then
d
matches role
i
. For a configuration
\mathbf{C}
such that
\mathbf{C}_{0}\xrightarrow{*}\mathbf{C}
and a valid assignment
A
, let
s_{A}(\mathbf{C})
be the set of correctly assigned colors in
\mathbf{C}
with respect to
A
,
i.e.

s_{A}(\mathbf{C})\coloneqq\{d\in\mathbb{D}\colon(\exists i\in[1..n]:A(i)=d)\land(\exists q\in Q_{L}:\mathbf{C}(d,q)>0\land\mathrm{role}(q)=i)\}.

Moreover, we say that a configuration
\mathbf{C}
is
fixed
if:

•

\mathbf{C}_{\gamma}\xrightarrow{*}\mathbf{C}
,

•

there exists
q\in\mathrm{act}(\mathbf{C})\cap Q_{C}
such that

\bigwedge_{i=1}^{n}\mathrm{task}_{i}(q)
, and

•

\mathrm{role}(q)\geq 0
for every
q\in\mathrm{act}(\mathbf{C})
.

Note that a strictly negative role can only be assigned by rule
(1)
(leader election), rule
(4)
(count change) and rule
(6)
(self-reassignment). Observe that rules
(1)
,
(4)
and
(6)
are disabled for a fixed configuration. We also know that the controller can only change one of its tasks to
\mathit{false}
if it observes a negative role through rule
(7)
or a negative controller through rule
(9)
. Since a fixed configuration does not have a strictly negative role, nor can it generate one, rule
(7)
cannot change any of the controller’s tasks to
\mathit{false}
. Furthermore, rule
(9)
is disabled for a fixed configuration. Hence, a fixed configuration can only reach fixed configurations.

Now, let
A
be a valid assignment. For any
v\geq\gamma
, we observe two cases.

Case:
\exists A^{\prime}:|s_{A^{\prime}}(\mathbf{C}_{v})|=n
.
There exists a fixed configuration
\mathbf{C}^{\prime}
, reachable from
\mathbf{C}_{v}
.

Case:
\forall A^{\prime}:|s_{A^{\prime}}(\mathbf{C}_{v})|<n
.
This implies that some role
i
is not taken by any leader. If
A(i)
’s leader has no assigned role, then by rule
(5)
there exists a configuration
\mathbf{C}^{\prime}
, reachable from
\mathbf{C}_{v}
, such that
|s_{A}(\mathbf{C}_{v})|<|s_{A}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime})|
and

\mathrm{role}(q)=i\text{ for some }q\in\mathrm{act}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime}(A(i)))\cap Q_{L}
.

Otherwise, we observe two cases:

•

The controller has its task
i
set to
\mathit{false}
, allowing self-reassignment of
A(i)
’s leader through rule
(6)
.

•

The controller has its task
i
set to
\mathit{true}
. Since role
i
is not taken by any leader, by
Lemma

28
, for the controller to have its task
i
set to
\mathit{true}
, some
q\in\mathrm{act}(\mathbf{C}_{v})\cap Q_{L}
satisfies
\mathrm{role}(q)=-i
. Thus, by rule
(7)
, there exists a configuration
\mathbf{C}^{\prime}
reachable from
\mathbf{C}_{v}
such that

\lnot\mathrm{task}_{i}(q)
for some
q\in\mathrm{act}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime})\cap Q_{C}
.
The latter allows self-reassignment of
A(i)
’s leader through rule
(6)
.

Consequently, there also exists a configuration
\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime}
, reachable
from
\mathbf{C}^{\prime}
, such that
|s_{A}(\mathbf{C}_{v})|<|s_{A}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime})|
and
\mathrm{role}(q)=i
for some
q\in\mathrm{act}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime}(A(i)))\cap Q_{L}
.

By fairness, since there exists a finite number of configurations and a finite number of valid assignments for
\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{C}_{0})
, either for some
v
,
|s_{A}(\mathbf{C}_{v})|=n
holds and, by fairness,
\mathbf{C}_{v}
leads to a fixed configuration
\mathbf{C}_{\tau}
; or for some
v
and some valid assignment
A^{\prime}\neq A
,
|s_{A^{\prime}}(\mathbf{C}_{v})|=n
holds, and again, by fairness, this leads to a fixed configuration
\mathbf{C}_{\tau}
. Since we know that fixed configurations can only reach fixed configurations and that they also guarantee the controller has its task list completely assigned, this is true for any
\tau^{\prime}\geq\tau
.
∎

Proof of
\Leftarrow
).

We prove the contrapositive. Assume
\psi(\mathbf{C}_{0})=\mathit{false}
. Let
W(\mathbf{C})
be the set of data
d\in\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{C}_{0})
such that for some
q\in\mathrm{act}(\mathbf{C}(d))\cap Q_{L}
,

•

|\mathrm{role}(q)|=i
holds for some
i\in[1..n]
, and

•

\mathbf{C}(d,Q_{j})\notin T(i,j)
holds for some
j\in[1..m]
.

Informally,
W(\mathbf{C})
is the set of data in
\mathbf{C}
with a leader having a wrongly assigned role (positive or negative),
i.e.
, a role the data cannot match. Since
|\mathbf{C}_{\beta}|_{Q_{L}}=|\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{C}_{0})|
and the number of leaders never increases, for any
\mathbf{C}^{\prime}
such that
\mathbf{C}_{\beta}\xrightarrow{*}\mathbf{C}^{\prime}
, we have
W(\mathbf{C}^{\prime})\leq|\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{C}_{0})|
.
Furthermore, by
Lemma

23
, the counts are stable and correct for leaders past
\mathbf{C}_{\beta}
. The latter implies that for any
t\geq\beta
and datum
d
, if a role
i
is assigned by rule
(5)
to
d
’s leader, then
d
matches role
i
. Therefore, for any
i,j\geq\beta
with
i\leq j
, we have
W(\mathbf{C}_{j})\leq W(\mathbf{C}_{i})
.

Assume that there exists some
\pi\geq\beta
such that
W(\mathbf{C}_{\pi})>0
. Then, for some
d\in\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{C}_{0})
, there exists a state
q\in\mathrm{act}(\mathbf{C}_{\pi}(d))\cap Q_{L}
that satisfies
|\mathrm{role}(q)|=i
, where
i
is a role
d
does not match. Since
\pi\geq\beta
, by
Lemma

23
, state
q
must satisfy
\bigwedge_{j\in[1..m]}\mathrm{count}_{j}(q)=\min(\mathbf{C}_{0}(d,Q_{j}),r)
,
i.e.
, it must have the right counts for each elements for
d
.

Thus, by
Lemma

24
,
\mathrm{role}(q)
must be strictly negative. This means that rule
(7)
is enabled in
\mathbf{C}_{\pi}
. So, there exist
\mathbf{C}^{\prime}
and
q\in\mathrm{act}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime})\cap Q_{C}
such that
\mathbf{C}_{\pi}\xrightarrow{*}\mathbf{C}^{\prime}
and
\lnot\mathrm{task}_{i}(q)
holds. Note that rule
(8)
is now enabled in
\mathbf{C}^{\prime}
. Therefore, there exists a configuration
\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime}
such that
\mathbf{C}^{\prime}\xrightarrow{(8)}\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime}
and
W(\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime})<W(\mathbf{C}_{\pi})
. Thus, by fairness, there exists
u\geq\pi
such that
W(\mathbf{C}_{u})=0
.

From
Lemma

28
, for any
v\geq\beta
, for all
q\in\mathrm{act}(\mathbf{C}_{v})\cap Q_{C}
, if
\mathrm{task}_{i}(q)
holds, then there exists some state
q^{\prime}\in\mathrm{act}(\mathbf{C}_{v})\cap Q_{L}
such that
\mathrm{role}(q^{\prime})=\pm i
. Recall from
Lemma

26
that any non-leader eventually has a role of
0
.
Furthermore, observe that

•

for
\mathbf{C}_{u}
described above, for any
d\in\mathbb{D}
, if
d
’s leader has an assigned role
i
, then
d
matches role
i
; and

•

for every
w\geq\max(u,\gamma)
, since
\psi(\mathbf{C}_{0})
is
\mathit{false}
, there is no valid assignment
A
such that
|s_{A}(\mathbf{C}_{w})|=n
.

Thus, no
\mathbf{C}_{w}
satisfies the following for all
i\in[1..n]
:

\exists d\in\mathrm{supp}(\mathbf{C}_{0}),q\in\mathrm{act}(\mathbf{C}_{w}(d))\cap Q_{L}:|\mathrm{role}(q)|=i.

Therefore, there exists
\tau\in\mathbb{N}
such that, for any
\mathbf{C}^{\prime}
with
\mathbf{C}_{\tau}\xrightarrow{*}\mathbf{C}^{\prime}
, there exists
i\in[1..n]
such that, for every
q\in\mathrm{act}(\mathbf{C}^{\prime})\cap Q_{C}
, it is the case that
\lnot\mathrm{task}_{i}(q)
holds.
∎

See
30

Proof.

Case:
\psi(\mathbf{C}_{0})=\mathit{true}
.
Let
Q_{\mathit{true}}\coloneqq\{q\in Q\colon\mathrm{out}(q)\}
. Note that only one controller exists for any configuration reachable from
\mathbf{C}_{\alpha}
. Recall the notion of fixed configurations from the proof of
Lemma

29
. For a fixed configuration
\mathbf{C}
, if there exists a state
q\in\mathrm{act}(\mathbf{C})
such that
\lnot\mathrm{out}(q)
holds, since the controller has its task list completely assigned, then there exists a configuration
\mathbf{C}^{\prime}
reachable from
\mathbf{C}
such that
|\mathbf{C}|_{Q_{\mathit{true}}}<|\mathbf{C}^{\prime}|_{Q_{\mathit{true}}}
. Note that the controller never propagates the
\mathit{false}
output in a fixed configuration; it follows that
|\mathbf{C}_{v}|_{Q_{\mathit{true}}}\leq|\mathbf{C}^{*}|_{Q_{\mathit{true}}}
for any
\mathbf{C}^{*}
reachable from
\mathbf{C}_{v}
. Invoking fairness, this implies that there also is a configuration
\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime}
reachable from
\mathbf{C}^{\prime}
such that
|\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime}|_{Q_{\mathit{true}}}=|\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime}|
. From the proof of
Lemma

29
, we also know that if
\psi(\mathbf{C}_{0})=\mathit{true}
, then a fixed configuration is eventually reached. Therefore, some configuration
\mathbf{C}_{\tau}
such that
O(\mathbf{C}_{\tau})=\mathit{true}
is reached; it remains
\mathit{true}
for any configuration
\mathbf{C}_{\tau^{\prime}}
such that
\mathbf{C}_{\tau}\xrightarrow{*}\mathbf{C}_{\tau^{\prime}}
.

Case:
\psi(\mathbf{C}_{0})=\mathit{false}
.
From
Lemma

29
, we know that for some
u\in\mathbb{N}
and for any
v\geq u
, at least one task is unassigned for the controller in
\mathbf{C}_{v}
. Assume that there exists some
v\geq u
such that some
q\in\mathrm{act}(\mathbf{C}_{v})
satisfies
\mathrm{out}(q)
. Then, since the controller has at least one task that is unassigned, rule
(11)
is enabled (with the controller propagating the
\mathit{false}
output) and there exists a configuration
\mathbf{C}^{\prime}
reachable from
\mathbf{C}_{v}
such that
|\mathbf{C}_{v}|_{Q_{\mathit{true}}}>|\mathbf{C}^{\prime}|_{Q_{\mathit{true}}}
. Note that the controller never propagates the
\mathit{true}
output for
\mathbf{C}_{v}
; it follows that
|\mathbf{C}_{v}|_{Q_{\mathit{true}}}\geq|\mathbf{C}^{*}|_{Q_{\mathit{true}}}
for any
\mathbf{C}^{*}
reachable from
\mathbf{C}_{v}
. Thus, by fairness, this implies that there exists a configuration
\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime}
reachable from
\mathbf{C}^{\prime}
such that
|\mathbf{C}^{\prime\prime}|_{Q_{\mathit{true}}}=0
. So, some configuration
\mathbf{C}_{\tau}
such that
O(\mathbf{C}_{\tau})=\mathit{false}
is reached. Any configuration
\mathbf{C}_{\tau^{\prime}}
reachable from
\mathbf{C}_{\tau}
also satisfies
O(\mathbf{C}_{\tau})=\mathit{false}
.
∎
